|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| RC1 | P | T6 | L0 | resonant_chains.TARGET_RATIOS 2:1, 3:2, 4:3, 5:3, the period ratios a pair is taken to be near | add 5:4, 5:2, 7:5 | multiplanet systems | a chain of measured planets near a ratio not in the list that forces a transit timing signal | the resonances of the observed multis (Fabrycky et al. 2014 ratio histogram) | the design note's list (`weird-worlds.md` W5) |
| RC2 | P | T6 | L0 | resonant_chains.RESONANCE_TOLERANCE 0.05, how near a ratio must be to a target | 0.02 to 0.08 | the same | a population whose period-ratio excess near resonance extends past 5 percent | the observed distribution of `Delta` of resonant pairs (Goldberg and Batygin 2022) | the design note's 5 percent gate; the Kepler multis cluster at +0.01 to +0.02, recalled |
| RC3 | P | T4 | L0 | resonant_chains.WIDTH_EXPONENT 2/3, the detuning is held at `mu ** (2/3)` or more | 0.5 to 0.8 | first-order resonances of planets | an N-body run in resonance whose eccentricity exceeds the capped value by more than a factor of two | the libration width of the resonance computed from the pendulum model | Lithwick, Xie and Wu 2012: the near-resonance formula holds for `Delta` above `mu^(2/3)`, recalled |
| RC4 | P | T6 | L1 | resonant_chains.CROSSING_SHARE 0.5, the share of the orbit-crossing limit the forced eccentricity is held to | 0.3 to 0.9 | chains of heavy planets | a stable chain with forced eccentricities over half the crossing limit | a stability integration of the chain (the overlap-free systems chain) | the necessary condition for non-crossing orbits; the share is ours |

### 6.93 Stand-ins of the collisional mantle stripping (GEN.1040)

`physics/mantle_stripping.py` is oddity W10: a head-on giant impact that blows off the rock and leaves an iron-rich planet (Mercury about 70 percent iron by mass, Kepler-107 c about 70 percent, an Earth-like planet a third). It is the law of Marcus, Stewart, Sasselov and Hernquist 2010 (ApJL 712, L73; arXiv:0907.0234, read) from the masses and the speed to the largest remnant: `x = Q_R / Q*_RD`, `M_lr / M_tot = 1 - x/2` (equation 2), the remnant's iron `0.33 + 0.25 x^1.65` (equation 3), `Q*_RD = 1e-4 R_C1^1.2 V^0.8` erg/g (equation 4). The oddity's rate is the registry row WR4 of 6.82. Checked against the one giant-impact run with every number printed (Bonomo and others 2019, Fig. 3), Kepler-107 b and c through the repository's mass-radius model, and Mercury, in `src/tests/test_mantle_stripping.py`. The module is not read by the generator until GEN.1048 wires W10 (and GEN.1047 the table).

| MT2 | D | T1 | L0 | the largest remnant `M_lr / M_tot = 1 - x/2` for `x` from 0 to 2 | none | the same | a remnant mass more than 25 percent from it at the same `x` | none | Marcus 2010 equation 2 (read), after Stewart and Leinhardt 2009 |
| MT3 | D | T1 | L0 | the iron fraction of the remnant `0.33 + 0.25 x^1.65` for bodies that began a third iron | none | `x` from 0 to about 1.8 (the rock is gone beyond it) | an SPH run of two Earth-like bodies whose remnant iron differs by more than 0.1 at the same `x` | none | Marcus 2010 equation 3 (read); Bonomo 2019 Fig. 3 run gives 0.64 where it gives 0.64 at x of 1.21 |
| MT4 | P | T1 | L2 | mantle_stripping.Q_STAR_SCALE 0.6, the factor on the threshold of MS1 for planet-sized bodies | 0.5 to 1 | head-on impacts of 1e24 to 1e26 kg | an SPH run whose remnant mass differs by more than 25 percent from the module at the same speed | a threshold fitted to more runs (Marcus and others, in preparation, not found) | Marcus 2010 section 3.1 (read): the data "fall short of the prediction from Equation 4 by less than a factor of 2"; Bonomo 2019 Fig. 3 (read): two 10.5 Earth masses at 62.5 km/s leave 8.2 Earth masses and 64 percent iron, which 0.6 reproduces (8.28 and 0.642); tuned to that one point |
| MT5 | P | T6 | L0 | the retention law for another starting core fraction: the iron and the rock kept are equation 3 and the remnant mass read as shares of a third-iron body, iron held to all the body had | none | core fractions 0.1 to 0.6 | an SPH run of a body that began at 0.15 or 0.5 iron whose remnant iron differs by more than 0.1 from the module | an SPH grid over starting core fractions | the form is derived from equations 2 and 3, equal to equation 3 at a third |

| MT7 | P | T1 | L0 | mantle_stripping.IMPACT_ANGLE_DEG 30, the angle from head-on beyond which an impact is a hit-and-run and strips nothing | 30 to 45 | similar-sized bodies | an SPH run of a 40 degree impact between embryos that strips more than a tenth of the mantle | the angle at which the disruption energy doubles, by mass ratio | Marcus 2010 section 3 (read): the onset of hit-and-run is at 30 to 45 degrees (Asphaug 2009); all their 45 degree cases were merging or hit-and-run; the share of impacts inside it is `sin^2` |

### 6.94 Stand-ins of the irregular zone (GEN.1076)

`physics/irregular_zone.py` gives where a captured small body can stay round a planet: the Hill radius (Jewitt and Haghighipour 2007 equation 1, 0.35, 0.43, 0.47 and 0.77 AU for the four giants), the time-averaged stable limits of a prograde and a retrograde orbit as shares of it, the Kozai band (`moon_composition.KOZAI_FORBIDDEN_DEG`) and the inner edge set by the outermost regular moon (Vokrouhlicky, Nesvorny and Levison 2008). Design `docs/design/systems/captured-asteroid-moons.md` 2.1 and 4.3. The module is not read by the generator until the captured-moon wiring.

| IZ2 | P | T1 | L0 | irregular_zone.RETROGRADE_STABLE_FRACTION 0.55, the share of the Hill radius out to which a retrograde orbit is stable | 0.5 to 0.6 | retrograde irregulars of the four giants | a stable retrograde body found beyond 0.6 of the Hill radius | the same integration-fitted limit | Vokrouhlicky, Nesvorny and Levison 2008, quoting Nesvorny et al. 2003: 0.5 to 0.6 depending on the mean eccentricity; 0.55 is the middle |

### 6.99 Stand-ins of the belt density factor (GEN.1093)

`physics/belt_density.py` gives a belt's objects per square AU above a stated diameter (1, 10 and 100 km on the information page) and its mass per square AU, from the belt's annulus, its stored mass, a broken power size law and the bodies the ledger keeps one by one: the tail is normalised to the mass the listed bodies leave, so the listed bodies and the tail add to the belt's mass exactly. The main belt law is Ivezic et al. 2001 read as differential exponents (2.3 below 5 km, 4 above, so cumulative 1.3 and 3.0); read as cumulative it leaves 3 asteroids above 40 km, the differential reading about 130 (the belt has some hundreds). With the nine biggest asteroids listed the Solar System's belt comes out at about 1.4 million objects above 1 km (Ivezic: 530,000) and about 120 above 100 km (some 200). Not read by the generator until UX.240.

### 6.96 Stand-ins of the irregular orbits (GEN.1078)

`physics/irregular_orbits.py` draws the orbits of captured small bodies before the irregular zone (6.94) cuts them: the size of the orbit over the Hill radius, its eccentricity, its inclination, and the fragments of a family. Design `docs/design/systems/captured-asteroid-moons.md` 2.1, 2.4 and 4.3. The module is not read by the generator until the captured-moon wiring. By stability alone the survivors split 4.5 retrograde to 1 prograde at a Jupiter-like planet (the known irregulars: 88 to 19) and about 1 to 1 at the far giants, so the excess retrograde moons of Uranus (8 to 1) are not modelled.

### 6.97 Stand-ins of the belt origins (GEN.1088)

`physics/belt_origin.py` gives every belt the cause that made it (design `belt-origins.md` sections 3 to 5): a gap in the planet chain, its unfinished tail, a pile-up at the snow line, the outer disc, the remains of a destroyed planet or rubble of a collision, and the two things that take mass afterwards, a giant's chaotic zone and resonance gaps and a giant that moved through. It stores the cause, the mass shares and the sentence. The values below are the ones the module holds; the numbers read in papers are the rows BR2 and BR4 of 6.95, which this table points to rather than repeats. The module is not read by the generator until GEN.1068 and GEN.1092.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| BG2 | P | T6 | L0 | belt_origin.MIN_BELT_RATIO 1.1, the least outer over inner distance of a belt | 1.05 to 1.3 | every belt | a belt of the ledger narrower than 10 percent in distance | the ledger's own minimum belt width | the ledger keeps `MIN_ASTEROID_BELT_SEPARATION` of 0.05 AU between a belt and a planet; 10 percent is the width at which one stretch is still a ring |

### 6.98 Stand-ins of the band winds (GEN.1110)

`physics/band_winds.py` estimates the mean surface wind by day and by night in each latitude band as an order of magnitude: the heat-engine speed `sqrt((dT/T) R_s dT)` of the day-night and equator-to-pole differences, the thermal wind `(R_s/f)(dT_ep/L) ln(P_s/P_top)` where the world turns fast, their smooth minimum, a share of it reaching the ground (less at night), the diurnal flow cut at high latitude by the inertial limit, the Hadley edge `sqrt(5/3 Ro_T)` to name the regime, and a fade below about 1 kPa. The Earth comes out at 4 to 12 m/s by day (mid-latitudes 5 to 7), Mars at 2 to 7, Venus under half a metre a second and Titan about 2. Not read by the generator until the seven-band climate (GEN.1108) feeds it.

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| BW1 | P | T9 | L2 | band_winds.PRESSURE_FADE_PA 1000 Pa, where the surface wind is half what the air aloft allows | 300 to 3000 | air under a few kilopascals | a measured surface wind on a thin-air world (Mars, Pluto, Triton) that the fade misses by more than a factor of three | a drag law from the air's density and the surface roughness | Boss's "about 1 kPa" in the item (GEN.1110) |
| BW2 | P | T9 | L3 | band_winds.PRESSURE_FADE_EXPONENT 2, how sharply the fade falls below its pressure | 1 to 4 | the same | Mars (600 Pa) and Pluto (1 Pa) winds both measured | the same drag law | chosen so Mars keeps a quarter of the wind aloft and Pluto nothing |
| BW3 | P | T6 | L1 | band_winds.THERMAL_WIND_LOG_PRESSURE 1.6, `ln(P_s / P_top)` of the thermal wind | 1.0 to 2.3 | worlds with a tropopause | a world whose tropopause is far from a fifth of the surface pressure (Venus, Titan) | the tropopause pressure of the radiative-convective solve | the Earth's tropopause is 0.2 of the surface pressure, recalled |
| BW4 | P | T9 | L3 | band_winds.SURFACE_COUPLING 0.3, the share of the wind aloft that reaches the surface by day | 0.15 to 0.5 | rocky worlds with air | surface winds off the Earth's 5 to 8 m/s in the mid-latitudes | a boundary-layer drag law on the surface roughness | tuned to the Earth's mid-latitude surface wind |
| BW5 | P | T9 | L3 | band_winds.NIGHT_COUPLING 0.5, the share of the day's coupling left at night | 0.3 to 0.8 | worlds with a stable night layer | a measured night to day wind ratio far from a half | a boundary-layer stability model | the Earth's night winds are about half the day's, recalled |

### 6.89 Stand-ins of the rogue planet drift (GEN.1114)

`physics/rogue_drift.py` gives where an ejected planet is relative to its star a time after the kick, by the exact solution of Hill's equations in the star's rotating frame (`x = (2 Omega v / kappa^2)(1 - cos kappa t) + (u / kappa) sin kappa t`, the slide along the orbit at `4 Omega A / kappa^2` times the along-orbit kick, the vertical oscillation at `nu`), with the velocity relative to the star and a ring form that turns the along-orbit offset into a wrapped azimuth. It agrees with a direct nonlinear orbit integration in a flat-rotation-curve potential to within a few percent of the separation out to 1 kpc and 200 Myr, and gives the design note's medians (about 30 pc after 10 Myr, 270 pc after 100 Myr for a 3 km/s isotropic kick). The kick is an input. Not read by the generator until the virtual-donor placement (GEN.1111).

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GD1 | P | T1 | L0 | rogue_drift.OORT_A_KM_S_KPC 15.3 km/s/kpc, the shear at the Sun | 14.8 to 15.8 | the Sun's neighbourhood | an Oort constant measured at another galactocentric radius | the potential's own `Omega` and `kappa` at the sector's radius (`Galaxy.from_frequencies`) | Bovy 2017, quoted in the drift note, section 3.1 |
| GD2 | P | T1 | L0 | rogue_drift.OORT_B_KM_S_KPC -11.9 km/s/kpc | -12.4 to -11.4 | the Sun's neighbourhood | the same | the same | Bovy 2017, quoted in the drift note |
| GD3 | P | T6 | L1 | rogue_drift.VERTICAL_FREQUENCY_KM_S_KPC 76 km/s/kpc, `nu = sqrt(4 pi G rho)` for 0.1 solar masses per cubic parsec | 60 to 90 | the solar neighbourhood | a vertical frequency from the potential differing by more than 10 percent (the repo's galactic-potential value at the Sun is 74.3) | the potential's `nu` at the sector's radius and height | the standard midplane-density estimate; galactic-potential.md section 7.1 |
| GD4 | P | T6 | L1 | rogue_drift.LINEAR_LIMIT_PC 1500, the separation up to which the linear equations are taken as good | 1000 to 2000 | all drifts | the integrator behind the potential disagreeing with the closed form by more than 10 percent inside this | the orbit integrator's own validity map | the drift note's "1 to 2 kpc"; the test against a nonlinear integration is good to 5 percent at 600 pc |

### 6.86 Stand-ins of the rogue planet top-up (GEN.1125)

`physics/rogue_topup.py` plans stage 3 of the region run (region-object-store section 8.5): from a sector's mass shortfall, the Rogues level and the stars inside it, how many rogue planets to add, each one's mass (a mass bin by its per-star rate, then dN/dlnM going as M^-slope inside the bin, so about 96 percent terrestrial), a uniform place inside the sector, a velocity through the Galaxy (its host's plus the kick of `rogue_kick` for a giant at a log-uniform distance, with the drift of `rogue_drift`) and an apparent origin marked "apparent", never a donor link. The count stops at the level's ceiling per star less the rogues already there, the last rogue is cut to the shortfall, and any mass left over is returned for the asteroid fields (GEN.1126), so the placed mass plus the remainder is the shortfall exactly. Not read by the generator until the region run wires it (GEN.1120).

### 6.88 Stand-ins of the rogue planet age (GEN.1112, RP5)

`physics/rogue_age.py` gives a rogue planet's age (the ejecting star's, drawn from the galaxy's star-formation `AgeLaw`), the delay after formation at which it was thrown out (power-law density in time from the shortest delay to the parent's age, an even share of each decade at index 1) and the time since ejection, the three numbers the heat law, the drift and the history sentence need. The ejection record of GEN.1119 replaces the drawn delay with the recorded one (`from_record`). Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

### 6.127 Stand-ins of the architecture's losses (GEN.1119, RP1)

`physics/scatter.py` (`loss_fates`, `reach_band_au`, `hill_radius_au`) and `planet_architecture.losses` share every body the architecture loses among ejected, collided and bound by the Safronov number of the encounter (design `docs/design/research/rogue-planet-review.md` sections 5 and 7). The Safronov number, the chaotic zone and the Hill radius are textbook laws; the two rows are the weights between the three fates and the time of a disc-era loss. The ejection record they return is what the rogue builder (GEN.1111) and the age module (`rogue_age`, row RG1) read.

### 6.87 Stand-ins of the rogue planet ejection speed (GEN.1112, RP6)

`physics/rogue_kick.py` gives the speed at infinity of a body ejected by a scattering planet, `v_inf = v_c X` with `v_c = 3 km/s (m_p / M_Jup)^(1/2) (a / 1 AU)^(-1/2) m_p / (m_p + m_ej)` (Huang and Lai 2025, Eq. 26) and `X` log-normal of mean 0.87 (Kramer and Profumo 2026, Sec. 4.1 to 4.2) and 99th percentile 2.5 (Huang and Lai Sec. 4.3); the Safronov number cut for which scatterers eject; the survival of an ejected planet's moons (Huang and Lai Eq. 4: a moon inside half the Hill sphere that the closest approach leaves it stays, the closest approach a log-normal of median 0.089 mutual Hill radii for 10 Jupiter masses ejecting 1, falling as the scatterer's mass to the 1.13); an isotropic direction (answered by Boss, 5:29 PM EDT 2026-10-30); and the rogue's velocity through the Galaxy as its host's plus the drift's relative velocity. GEN.1113 replaced the earlier energy-step guess (RK1 of the first build) with these: a Jupiter scattering from 5.2 AU gives a mean of 1.15 km/s, a 10-Jupiter-mass scatterer 3.5, and the median over giants of 0.3 to 10 Jupiter masses at 1 to 30 AU is inside the 0.7 to 10.7 km/s of Sayed, Profumo and Smyth. The tail that eccentric giants and three-planet systems add is not modelled. Rogues of thin-disc hosts pass the Sun at a median of about 40 km/s, with 'Oumuamua's 26 and Borisov's 32 km/s inside the 10 to 90 percent window. Reading and numbers: `docs/design/research/rogue-ejection-kick.md`. Not read by the generator until the rogue rows (GEN.1111).

### 6.110 Stand-ins of the masses of ejected bodies (GEN.1112, RP2)

`physics/rogue_mass_function.py` gives the masses of the bodies a system throws out as the supply of a collisional cascade, `dN/dlogM ~ M^-(q - 1)/3` with Dohnanyi's `q = 3.5` (0.83; 1.0 at the `q = 4` of Pan and Schlichting 2012), cut at the mass of the scatterer, the lighter of a pair being the one ejected. It replaces the fitted slope 0.65, the four hand-set bins and the 0.1 Earth-mass floor of `ROGUE_PLANET_MASS_BINS`. Sumi et al. 2023 measure 0.96 (+0.47 -0.27) for the free-floating planets and the module's 0.83 sits inside it without being fitted to it. It gives the shape, the shares of the ejected bodies in a mass range and a draw; the count and the total mass come from the ejection record (GEN.1119). Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

| RM2 | P | T6 | L0 | rogue_mass_function.MAX_EJECTED_OVER_SCATTERER 1.0, the heaviest body a scatterer ejects as a multiple of its own mass | 0.3 to 1 | scatterers that eject (Safronov number over 1) | an N-body run in which a scatterer ejects a body heavier than itself, or lets a body under a tenth of its mass stay | the ejection records (GEN.1119), which give every ejected body's mass beside its scatterer's | the lighter of a pair is the one ejected (the architecture's `_scatter_crowded_giants` already does it); Barclay et al. 2017 arXiv:1704.08749 eject embryos of at most one Earth mass beside Jupiter and Saturn analogues, read |

### 6.111 Stand-ins of the air and water of ejected planets (GEN.1112, RP3)

`physics/rogue_volatiles.py` replaces the coins for a rogue's hydrogen envelope (`ROGUE_HYDROGEN_ENVELOPE_CHANCE`, `ROGUE_HYDROGEN_ENVELOPE_PRESSURE_BAR`), its water (`ROGUE_WATER_RICH_CHANCE`, `ROGUE_WATER_MASS_FRACTION_RANGE`), its frozen air (`ROGUE_FROZEN_ATMOSPHERE_MIN_MASS_EARTH`) and its gas-giant class (`ROGUE_PLANET_GAS_GIANT_MASS_THRESHOLD_JUPITER`) with the laws the in-system planets already use, asked with the ejected planet's record: the XUV loss of `envelope_escape` over the ejection delay, the weight of the envelope on the surface for its pressure, the generator's own giant radius (`tuning.GIANT_MIN_RADIUS_KM`) on the radius law of Lopez and Fortney, the water of `delivered_water` at the snow-line distance it formed at, and the shoreline test of `atmosphere_retention` for an air. Two new choices, below. Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

### 6.112 Stand-ins of the moons carried out with ejected planets (GEN.1112, RP4)

`physics/rogue_moons.py` gives whether a moon is still bound when its planet is thrown out and what it does to the planet afterwards. A moon stays when its orbital time is shorter than the closest encounter's, `a_moon < r_min (m_p / (m_p + m_s))^(1/3)` (Huang and Lai 2026 eq. 4); the closest approach is the ejection record's (GEN.1119) or, without it, drawn from the two points their runs print (48 percent beyond 0.1 and 82 percent beyond 0.01 mutual Hill radii). A surviving moon is on an orbit of eccentricity 0.13 (Debes and Sigurdsson 2007) and raises a constant-eccentricity tide in the planet, about 5e-3 W/m2 for their median pair with their rheology and 1e-3 with the rock of `tidal_heating`. It replaces `ROGUE_TIDAL_FLUX_RANGE_W_M2` and the unasked question of which rogues kept a moon. Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MO1 | P | T2 | L1 | rogue_moons.SURVIVAL_AT_0P01_HILL 0.82 and SURVIVAL_AT_0P1_HILL 0.48: the shares of ejections whose closest approach stayed beyond 0.01 and 0.1 mutual Hill radii | 0.7 to 0.9 and 0.4 to 0.55 | a planet of 10 Jupiter masses ejecting one of 1 (m1 / M about 0.01, m1 / m2 = 10) round a solar-mass star; fewer survive for a lighter scatterer and a smaller mass ratio (their section 4.2.1 to 4.2.4) | the CCDF of a run with another mass ratio or inclination (their Figures 2 to 5 read for other values) | the ejection record's own closest approach (GEN.1119) | Huang and Lai 2026 arXiv:2508.18239 conclusions, read; the median 0.089 mutual Hill radii agrees with the two points |
| MO2 | P | T6 | L3 | rogue_moons.closest_approach_survival: the two points joined on a logarithmic axis and held to 0 to 1, so the survival is 1 under 0.003 and 0 over 1.9 mutual Hill radii | the slope of 0.34 per decade, the end points | between and just outside the two printed points | the CCDF of the paper's own simulations at 0.001 and 1 mutual Hill radius | the closest approach of the ejection record (GEN.1119), which needs no curve | the line through the two points; the ends are our extension |
| MO3 | P | T3 | L1 | rogue_moons.EJECTED_MOON_ECCENTRICITY 0.13: the eccentricity of a moon carried out with its planet | 0.05 to 0.3 | a lunar-mass moon at 30 Earth radii, a Jupiter as scatterer | a different moon mass or orbit whose ejected eccentricity is far from 0.13 in N-body runs | the moon's eccentricity from the ejection record (GEN.1119) | Debes and Sigurdsson 2007 arXiv:0709.0945, median of the 123 bound ejections, read (one setup, 2,700 runs) |
| MO4 | P | T6 | L1 | rogue_moons.circularisation_flux_w_m2: the eccentricity tide is held constant over the age, and the larger early term of the planet's spin being braked by the moon (their eq. 2) is not in it | e from 0.05 to 0.3 | the first hundreds of Myr after the ejection | a rogue younger than 300 Myr whose measured heat flux is far above the constant term | the spin-braking term from the spin-down work (GEN.842) | Debes and Sigurdsson 2007 eqs. 1 and 2: the spin term is 100 times the Earth's radiogenic heat for the best case and falls to it in 144 Myr (rocky) or 246 Myr (icy); the constant term is `tidal_heating`'s habit |

### 6.113 Stand-ins of the planets a birth cluster strips (GEN.1112, RP7; GEN.291)

`physics/cluster_unbinding.py` gives the share of planets of an orbit a cluster has unbound: the timescale `3 V / (40 pi G rho a)` of Spurzem et al. 2009 (eq. 37, from the impulsive cross section of eq. 36), applied through a density and speed history of the cluster. 22 Myr at 1,000 AU in 100 solar masses per cubic parsec at 2 km/s. It stands beside the rate of rogues per star and does not yet add to it. Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

| CU2 | B | T2 | L0 | cluster_unbinding.relative_speed_km_s: sqrt(6) times the one-axis dispersion, the rms relative speed of two stars from a Maxwellian | none | a cluster in virial equilibrium with isotropic motions | a cluster with a measured relative speed far from sqrt(6) sigma | the speed of encounters from the cluster's velocity distribution | the sum of two independent Maxwellians (our derivation) |

### 6.114 Stand-ins of the radiogenic heat of a rogue (GEN.1134)

`physics/rogue_radiogenic.py` gives a rogue rock the thorium, uranium, potassium and aluminium-26 of the star it left, by the laws a bound planet of that star uses: `radiogenic.host_offset_dex` for the star's [Fe/H], age and draw key, `rprocess_enrichment.th_u_factor_of_shape` for the star's age in its galaxy, and `radiogenic.draw_content` for the rest, so a rogue and a bound rocky planet of one star given one random stream have the same content. It replaces the host-less draw (`draw_content(None)`) in `rogue_surface`. The activity cut, tried as a mantle state, did not discriminate (the one-box mantle of `interior_heat` stays at 1,950 K or more for 0.01 to 3 Earth masses at 4.5 to 10 Gy), so the heat-flow cut stays, now registered. Not read by the generator until the rogue rows are created (GEN.1111).

### 6.119 Stand-ins of the composition of captured bodies (GEN.1080)

`physics/captured_composition.py` gives what a captured small body is made of: the taxon kernel (GEN.473, through `belt_member`) at the distance of its source, the outer planetesimal disc for an instability or exchange capture and the planet's neighbourhood for a gas-era one, then the water ice a capture takes away (the existing captured-moon rule). A family's parent is several lumps from different distances and a fragment is one lump, so members differ. The kernel's own stand-ins (TK1 to TK6) and the belt member's (MC1 to MC3) are not repeated. Not read by the generator until the captured-moon wiring (GEN.1083).

### 6.124 Stand-ins of the wiring of aluminium-26 at birth (GEN.476, GEN.998)

`radiogenic.al26_mean` gives a star's galaxy-wide mean 26Al/27Al from the yield of massive stars over the gas depletion time of the thin disc. The depletion time is the generator's own disc history (GEN.469), not a measurement. Trace (GEN.998): the default galaxy shape is a small one (5.8e9 solar masses of thin disc, scale length 772 pc), whose gas over star formation, rings weighted by area, is 0.85 Gy; a Milky Way mass disc (3.5e10, 2600 pc) gives 1.46 Gy, against 4.2 Gy measured for the Galaxy (0.7e10 solar masses of cold gas over 1.65 solar masses a year, Bland-Hawthorn and Gerhard 2016, Licquia and Newman 2015). The gap is the star formation law: Kennicutt's law at index 1.4 is applied to every ring with no low-density threshold, so the outer, gas-poor rings form stars faster than observed. The quantity is the right one (total gas over total star formation is the mean 26Al abundance's denominator); its value is a factor of about 3 short for a Milky Way mass disc, so the mean 26Al/27Al is about 3 times high.

### 6.130 Stand-ins of the decay exponent after X-ray saturation (GEN.493 wiring)

`activity.generate_activity` takes a star's saturation age from `saturation_time` (RS1, Johnstone et al. 2021) by its mass and its own uniform draw of the initial-rotation percentile, in place of the old table's age and the 0.3 dex scatter. The old table's decay exponents were calibrated with the old ages (an exponent of 2.0 with 0.10 Gyr gives the Sun 1.4e27 erg/s today); with the median age of 23 Myr the same exponent would give the Sun a twentieth of that. The exponents from 0.45 solar masses up are therefore the ones that hold each mass's `L_X/L_bol` at 5 Gyr where the earlier table put it. At one solar mass that is 1.45, inside the Skumanich band of 1.35 to 1.5, and the Sun comes out at 1.3e27 erg/s; Hyades-age G stars come out at 6.5e-6, against about 1e-5 observed (the earlier table gave 2.1e-5).

<!-- decay-exponent-standins:begin -->
| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AB1 | P | T9 | L3 | activity.DECAY_EXPONENTS: the exponent b of `L_X/L_bol` after saturation by mass (1.0 up to 0.3 solar masses, 1.35 at 0.45, 1.8 at 0.6, 1.45 at 0.8 and 1.0), tuned so each mass keeps its `L_X/L_bol` at 5 Gyr from the earlier table | each value within 0.3 of the one that reproduces the earlier level; the solar-mass 1.45 inside the Skumanich band | 0.45 to 1.2 solar masses and ages past the saturation age | a measured `L_X/L_bol` of field stars at 1 to 10 Gyr by mass differing from the model's by more than 0.5 dex, or a decay slope of the Johnstone et al. 2021 model for the same masses outside 1.0 to 2.0 | the decay of the rotation and X-ray model of Johnstone, Bartel and Guedel 2021 (the spin-down torque of Johnstone 2015, not read) | test_activity::test_the_decay_exponents_hold_the_old_table_level_at_the_reference_age |
<!-- decay-exponent-standins:end -->

## 7. Gemini's first batch

### 7.1 Counts and recommendation

The paste of 10:52 AM EDT is about 34,000 characters. I count about 110 distinct factual claims (numbers, relations, attributions) and about 45 that can be checked against a paper reachable by us. I checked 20 against papers or computations already in hand: **8 agree, 7 are contradicted by a verified figure, 5 are unsupported, misattributed or internally inconsistent.** A full claim-by-claim check is its own job: I recommend one separate checking thread that works the other 25 checkable claims by opening the cited papers, and a second only if Boss wants the unverifiable theory content (the theory frontiers section, about 30 claims) screened for factual errors.

The paste is a copy of the document the thread received and sits unchanged at `/mnt/project-files/reports/gap-filling-plan-2026-10-17-files/gemini-batch-1-unverified.txt`.

### 7.2 The 20 claims

| # | Gemini, unverified | Check | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Halo star counts reach no lower than M* about 3.8e10 | the six measured haloes span 3.8e10 to 8e10 (Harmsen 2017, READ in GEN.406) | agrees |
| 2 | Ex situ fraction rises steeply with M*, about 0.1 at Milky Way mass | Rodriguez-Gomez 2016 abstract (READ): about 0.10 at that mass, rising | agrees |
| 3 | Scale height ratio thick/thin 2.5 to 3.8 | Yoachim and Dalcanton: 2.5 ± 30 percent (READ); ranges overlap | agrees |
| 4 | Scale length ratio 1.0 to 1.3 | Yoachim and Dalcanton formula gives 0.95 to 1.18 over 89 to 255 km/s | agrees |
| 5 | Vertical heating index 0.46 ± 0.03 | Sharma and Hayden 2020 Table 2: 0.441 ± 0.007 | agrees |
| 6 | Dispersions rise linearly with height; low- and high-alpha follow the same law; 3 to 20 kpc | Sharma and Hayden 2020 abstract, same wording as Gemini's sentence (Gemini cites "Sharma et al. 2021 GALAH DR3"; the paper read is Sharma and Hayden 2020) | agrees (attribution loose) |
| 7 | Mean cluster half-light radius 2.7 ± 0.3 pc, no dependence on cluster luminosity | Jordan 2005 abstract: 2.7 ± 0.35 pc, none on luminosity; but the paper finds dependence on host distance, surface brightness and colour, so "invariant across host luminosity" overstates | agrees (partly) |
| 8 | Blue clusters about 20 percent larger than red | Jordan 2005: red about 17 percent smaller, i.e. blue/red = 1.20 | agrees |
| 9 | Occupation half-point 10^8.6 Msun | Benitez-Llambay and Frenk 2020: 10^9.4 Msun/h for early reionization, only 4 times lower for z_re = 4, i.e. 10^8.8 at the extreme | contradicted |
| 10 | Occupation reaches 1 at 10^9.3 and 0 at 10^7.8 | the same paper: none below 3e8 (10^8.5), all above 5e9 (10^9.7) | contradicted |
| 11 | Thick/thin mass ratio log = 0.35 - 1.10 log(Vc/100) | gives 2.5, 1.2, 0.8 at 89, 181, 255 km/s, above the hard bound of 0.96, 0.64, 0.54 (4.4); also gives 0.94 at 220 km/s while the same passage says 0.2 to 0.45 above 200 | contradicted |

| 13 | sigma_R(10 Gyr) = 62.0 ± 2.5 km/s | same table: 39.4 ± 0.3 | contradicted |
| 14 | Radial heating index 0.42 ± 0.03 | same table: 0.251 ± 0.006 | contradicted |
| 15 | Nuclear cluster mass slope 0.65 ± 0.10, attributed to Neumayer 2020 | Neumayer 2020 gives 0.48; Georgiev gives 1.0 to 1.36 (both READ in GEN.406); 0.65 matches neither | contradicted |

| 18 | Low-mass stellar-halo relation slope 1.90, zero point 6.80, from UniverseMachine | Behroozi 2019 Appendix J fits only 10^10.5 to 10^15 (READ), so the paper has no low-mass parameterisation to cite | misattributed |
| 19 | Thick-disc start 12 to 13 Gyr lookback at z = 2.5 to 4.0 (Pinna 2019) | with Planck-like cosmology 12 Gyr is z = 3.5 and 13 Gyr is z = 6.8 (computed), and 2.5 to 4 is 11.2 to 12.3 Gyr; Pinna 2019 not located by search | inconsistent, unsupported |
| 20 | Halo formation redshift z50 = 1.20 - 0.35 log(M/1e12), from Aquarius, Cooper 2010, Deason 2016, D'Souza and Bell 2018 | Cooper 2010 haloes are Milky Way mass only; no relation of this form located in the sources we hold | unsupported |

Not counted: the occupation width 0.25 ± 0.04 dex (agrees with the 0.20 to 0.25 derived from the verified edges, 4.5) is in the same row as the contradicted centre and is kept in the bracket for GR15 as a LEAD that agrees.

### 7.2a Result of the checking thread (2026-10-17)

The checking thread finished at 11:49 AM EDT as it reported ([gemini-batch1-check.md](gemini-batch1-check.md)). It checked 51 claims (the 20 above plus 31 more): 10 agree, 17 partly, 16 contradicted by a read source, 4 unsupported, 4 misattributed. Five of the 20 above moved after more reading. The corrections that matter for the build items: the occupation centre is 10^9.33 Msun/h with width 0.21 dex (Gemini's 10^8.6 is the paper's no-reionization limit); the 1.9 low-mass stellar-to-halo slope is Garrison-Kimmel 2014 (1.92), not UniverseMachine; the halo formation redshift z50 is 1.02 at 1e10 and 0.74 at 1e12 (Gemini 1.9 and 1.2); the age-velocity normalisations are 21.1 and 39.4 km/s with indices 0.441 and 0.251 (Gemini 38.5, 62.0, 0.46, 0.42); the Courteau scatter is 0.097 dex about the paper's own line (79 markers digitised from its Fig 2; the 64 galaxies were a misreading). Requests now answered by a usable table: R2 (all three curves), R5, R12 (the z_re table with Busha's spread), R13 (Munoz ultra-faint sizes) and parts of R3, R6 and R10. Still open for Boss's list: R1, R4 beyond the Milky Way, R7, R8, R14 and parts of R6, R9, R10, R11. Three conflicts between two verified sources (thick-to-thin height ratio 2.5 Yoachim and Dalcanton against 4.2 Comerón 2018 median; thick-to-thin mass ratio against Vc; cluster system radius about 3.5 against about 6 times the galaxy's) are Decided, widen and keep both (Boss's cards, tapped 12:02 PM EDT (read from the session log and relayed in words by the coordinator)): the height ratio is drawn from 2.5 to 4.2, the mass ratio against Vc is widened between the two trends, the system radius ratio is drawn from about 3.5 to about 6, and every source stays in the record.

### 7.3 How results enter

Every Gemini figure is stored as a LEAD record next to the fill it concerns, with the claim number from 7.2, the verified figure it was checked against and a verdict. Agreeing claims raise the fill's evidence only if a verified source is also cited; contradicted claims are kept and show in the loader report as "LEAD rejected". The checking thread of 7.1 turns the remaining checkable claims into verdicts of the same shape; the second batch Boss expects uses the same intake. The theory frontier content (WDM, SIDM, fuzzy dark matter, thick-disc origin paradigms, assembly bias) is not a measurement and is never a default; it can only supply the form of a T-kind fill, for example the gas-rich turbulent collapse picture behind GR6, and it is labelled theory wherever it appears.

Which gaps block what, as far as I traced:

- **Block the 8.1 headline chain**: the two gaps named in GEN.301's own prerequisite line, GR4 (through GEN.447) and GR9c (through GEN.280). GR4 is derived and ready (X2). GR9c is a bracket with a virial-theorem part and one reading job (X4); it can ship as a labelled bracket without waiting for the review. I did not trace the other prerequisites of GEN.301 to gaps, so there may be more.
- **Block the 8.1 stamp** (the six-phase "Galaxy laws build" items listed in the lane report): all galaxy rows of 5.1, in the order X2, X3, X4, X5. Boss decided items 1, 2, 6 and 7 of section 8, and each has a fill that ships without further answers (item 6 also runs the quick VizieR search first).
- **Do not block 8.1**: every body and atmosphere row. They belong to 9.0, with the generation changes that alter stored values staying with 8.4 as already Decided; X6 is the exception worth pulling early because its outputs (the star regression and the water-share function) are inputs to GEN.472 phase 2 and the 8.1 back-scattered building blocks.
- **Epoch**: a fill that replaces a value later changes stored output and rides the pending epoch; the X1 registry records the epoch per fill so the builder of GEN.322-324 style rescatters can see which stand-ins sat inside it.

## 10. Limits of this report

- Nothing in the repository was changed. No papers were taken from refused publishers. Every number marked COMPUTED can be reproduced with the scripts in the files folder.
- READ means a thread opened the paper or table; abstracts only are marked "abstract READ". The Jordan 2005 and Rodriguez-Gomez 2016 statements rest on abstracts. Nothing in 7.2 is more than one check deep.
- The accreted-halo integral is a sketch: it applies the Moster z = 0 relation at the accretion mass, ignores satellite stripping, and over-predicts by a factor of 2 to 3 against Illustris. It is a shape source and a test, not a law.
- The oxygen-budget water share has no disk chemistry. The age-velocity rule has one closure target (the Milky Way) and so cannot yet be tested on a second galaxy.
- The Brewer 2016 regressions use the whole catalogue without its quality cut; the cut must be applied by the builder (4.7).
- Gemini claim counts (110 distinct, 45 checkable) are my estimates from reading the paste; the 20 checked are exactly those in 7.2.

Files: `/mnt/project-files/reports/gap-filling-plan-2026-10-17-files/` holds `water_share.py`, `acc.py`, `ws_stars.py`, `arx.py`, `brewer2016_vizier_J_ApJS_225_32.tsv` and `gemini-batch-1-unverified.txt`.

As built (GEN.498): `physics/oxygen_fugacity.py` fixes the one oxygen-fugacity scale, the Hirschmann 2021 iron-wustite buffer, and every stored delta-IW is on it (`mantle_delta_iw` was already read that way: Earth's upper mantle at IW+3.5 is QFM, which stands 3.1 to 3.7 above IW). Checked against the papers Boss supplied (9:55 PM to 10:17 PM EDT on 2026-10-22, from the coordinator's wake times): Hirschmann's Table 1 (every coefficient, sign and the iron-phase boundary), the NASA Glenn coefficients of CO, CO2, H2, H2O and O2, and Sossi et al. 2020 equation 4 are verified; the stand-ins that remain, each a row of `oxygen_fugacity.SOURCES`: the Frost 1991 FMQ and NNO constants (the text is paywalled; the form is confirmed by Anenburg 2026 and their gap at 1 GPa agrees with it, the absolute values are second hand from PetroBuffer), and the Sossi scale's 0.5 offset (derived from his gas ratios; his IW formula is in his Supplementary Materials, not read; Hirschmann's JANAF offset explains its size). Nothing stored changes.

As built (GEN.470, row BC12): `physics/saturation.py` and `physics/data/saturation_pressure.json` hold the one vapour-pressure function `saturation(species, T)`, in Pa with a status (`ok`, `cold-unverified` below 50 K, `below-fit` under the polynomial's own turning point, `above-triple`). It carries the Fray and Schmitt 2009 polynomials for Ne, N2, CO, Ar, O2, CH4, Kr, Xe, C2H6, C2H4, C2H2, H2S, CO2, NH3, SO2, HCN, CH3OH and C6H6 as quoted in the supplementary table of Lisse et al. (2ND-HAND; O2-3 and Ne-2 from part 01 section 7.2) and water by Feistel and Wagner (76.03 Pa at 250 K, 0.1626 Pa at 200 K, 611.657 Pa at the triple point). **Stand-ins, because the paper's per-fit windows are not read (BC12 stays open):** where a species has more than one fit the next takes over where the two cross (N2 36.1 K, CO 60.75 K, O2 23.15 and 43.79 K, CH3OH 157.55 K, Ne 13.26 K; each within 1 K of the known solid transition) or come closest (CO2 195.3 K, 1.1 percent apart); every `floor_k` is derived from the polynomial. CO2-1 serves the cold, not the CO2-2 that part 01 recommends from its triple-point error alone, because CO2-2 reaches 1e-9 bar at 64.7 K against the 86 K the latent heat of sublimation gives; H2S-1 alone, as part 01 does. **Known gaps:** at the triple point SO2 is 3.8 percent over the gas table's pressure, benzene 7 percent over and acetylene 3.6 percent under, every other species within 3 percent; H2 and He have no curve; above the triple point the liquid branch of the next paragraph takes over. GEN.497 replaces the cold end and the solid CO2 curve; GEN.452 makes the atmosphere read the function.

As built (GEN.497, row BC12, the cold end): `saturation` now serves five species from the laboratory fits `ln P[bar] = A - B/T + C ln T + D2 T + D3 T^2 + D4 T^3` (Lobo and Ferreira 2001) of Grundy et al. 2024 (N2, CO, CH4) and Blakley et al. 2024 (O2, NH3) instead of the Fray and Schmitt polynomial in the cold, and carbon dioxide from Span and Wagner 1996 equation 3.12 for the whole solid (842.65 Pa at 150 K, 103971 Pa at 195 K, 101325 Pa at 194.6855 K; the two Fray and Schmitt CO2 fits are gone). Each laboratory fit is used from the bottom of its fit range (N2 20 K, CO 25, CH4 30, O2 30, NH3 80) up to where it meets the polynomial (N2 35 K, the top of its range, with a published step to the alpha polynomial of a factor of 2.1; CO 54.4 K; CH4 51.0; O2 42.7; NH3 162.3); inside it the status is `ok`, under it the curve is Clausius-Clapeyron with the latent heat the fit has at its edge, flagged `below-fit`, and above it the polynomial keeps `cold-unverified` below 50 K. `tuning.COLD_END_LABORATORY_CORRECTION` (on) is the switch for the 2026 Grundy preprint, which multiplies all five by 1.19 +/- 0.03: on is its Table 3 A and B with the 2024 constants C to D4 (N2, CO and CH4: the reconstructions of part G4, max 0.6 percent), off is the 2024 set (N2, CO and CH4: the same curve divided by 1.19). Both sets and their hand-over temperatures are in the data file. **Stand-ins, labelled:** the fit range of each curve is the range of Grundy's data package and Blakley's data (finding 21 of the atmosphere composition research lists the narrower measured windows, N2 23 to 31 K, CO 26 to 35, CH4 31 to 43, O2 29 to 37, NH3 88 to 105); the Clausius-Clapeyron continuation below the range has no width stated (the "doubled in width" of the BC12 decision is not carried yet); the N2 step; the C to D4 of N2, CO and CH4 are reconstructions. BC12 (the original per-fit windows) stays open.

As built (GEN.452, the liquid branch of `saturation`): above the triple point and up to the critical point `saturation` returns the Wagner fits to the NIST saturation tables of part 01 section 6.2 (COMPUTED by the Atmosphere research lane, within 0.26 percent of the table), hydrogen cyanide the Perry DIPPR 101 correlation, and water the Wagner fit (3538.6 Pa at 300 K against the steam table's 3536.8). Above the critical temperature the status is `above-critical` and the pressure is infinite; acetylene has no liquid curve. Hydrogen is a Clausius-Clapeyron solid from its triple point with 1.02 kJ/mol (0.90 vapourisation from the gas table plus 0.12 fusion, RECALLED, a stand-in unchecked against Woolley 1948). The solid and liquid curves meet within the part-01 spread (N2 -2.7 percent, CH4 and H2S +1.7, NH3 +1.9, HCN +3.3, SO2 +3.8, benzene +7.1, others within 1 percent), so a step at the triple point is documented, not smoothed.

As built (GEN.452, the swap): `physics/atmosphere.py` no longer holds a vapour-pressure constant. `CONDENSATION_K` and the `h2s` copy line are gone and `vapour_pressure_kpa` reads `saturation.pressure_pa` (infinite above the critical temperature, so a hot world caps nothing), and `saturation.condensation_temperature_k(species, pressure)` gives the inverse (bisection, `None` above the critical pressure). The twelve other rows of the checked table of part 01 section 7.4 (H2, N2, CO, Ar, O2, CH4, SO2, H2O, H2S, NH3, C2H6, HCN at seven pressures from 100 kPa to 1 mPa) were reproduced to 0.12 K by the curves as they stood when the table was made; after the laboratory cold ends of GEN.497 and the Span and Wagner carbon dioxide curve the test allows 1 K above 60 K and 2 K below. **The thirteenth, CO2, departs from that table on purpose:** the table used CO2-2, which is 4 K cold at 100 Pa and 17 K cold at 1 mPa; the carbon dioxide curve is now the Span and Wagner sublimation equation (GEN.497). The cold gases (N2, CO, CH4) follow the laboratory cold ends, up to about 1.6 K from the table below 60 K; the test tolerates that. N2 at 37 K is 1.2 Pa (Pluto's air, about 1 Pa), where the old table gave 10 Pa. The rogue-planet nitrogen frost (`rogue_surface.nitrogen_vapor_pressure_pa`) and the water curve of `hydrosphere.py` still hold their own constants; moving them is a stored-value change for GEN.461 and the rogue-surface item.


### 6.71 Stand-ins of the resonance rings of small bodies and the ring gate near a star (GEN.1056)

`physics/small_body_rings.py` puts a narrow ring at the orbit that goes round `RESONANCE_ORDER` (3) times slower than a small body turns (Quaoar 1/3, Haumea 3:1, Chariklo near it; the design note's cases, 2ND-HAND), past the Roche limit of its ice and inside half its Hill radius, with a chance `RESONANCE_RING_RATE`; and refuses any ring inside `INNER_RING_LIMIT_AU`. The test points are the three known rings, never inputs: the resonance radius comes out within 5 percent of Quaoar's and Haumea's ring and 10 percent of Chariklo's (its mass is the least certain).

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| RR1 | P | T4 | L3 | small_body_rings.RESONANCE_RING_RATE 0.10 and RESONANCE_ORDER 3 (the chance that a small body that meets the conditions has the resonance ring, and the order of the resonance; three of the roughly twenty bodies mapped by occultation have one, a selection-biased count) | rate 0.03 to 0.3; order 3 only | dwarf planets and centaurs under 5e22 kg | a survey of ring searches whose detection rate on bodies of this kind differs from 0.03 to 0.3, or a ring at another order | the survey statistic of the occultation ring searches (Morgado et al. 2023 and later) | test_small_body_rings::test_the_chance_is_the_registered_rate; test_small_body_rings::test_the_ring_orbits_three_times_slower_than_the_body_turns_where_the_known_rings_are |
| RR2 | P | T3 | L0 | small_body_rings.INNER_RING_LIMIT_AU 0.1 (no ring inside this distance from the star; Schlichting and Chang 2011 via the design note, 2ND-HAND, 'about 0.1 AU') | 0.05 to 0.3 | planets and small bodies round Sun-like stars | a ring measured round a planet inside 0.05 AU, or the lifetime calculation putting the limit outside 0.3 AU | the lifetime of a ring against the star's tides and heat, scaled with the star's luminosity | test_small_body_rings::test_no_ring_survives_inside_a_tenth_of_an_au |
| RR3 | P | T2 | L0 | small_body_rings.HILL_FRACTION_MAX 0.5 (the widest circular prograde orbit round a small body that stays bound, in Hill radii; Hamilton and Burns 1991, Domingos et al. 2006: 0.49) | 0.3 to 0.5 | prograde circular orbits | a stable prograde orbit measured outside 0.5 Hill radii | an N-body stability limit for the body's eccentricity | test_small_body_rings::test_a_resonance_outside_the_stable_zone_is_no_ring |
| RR4 | P | T6 | L3 | small_body_rings.RESONANCE_RING_MASS_KG (1e10, 1e14) and RESONANCE_WIDTH_FRACTION (0.002, 0.05) (the ring's mass and its width over its radius, log-uniform; the range gives a mean optical depth from 0.001 to 10 over a thin ring at a few thousand km) | mass one decade each way; width 0.001 to 0.1 | narrow rings of small bodies | an optical depth and a width measured for Quaoar's ring outside the range | the mass from the ring's optical depth and width as measured by occultations | test_small_body_rings::test_the_ring_is_thin_light_and_dense_enough_to_see |

### 6.73 Stand-ins of the disintegrating rocky planet (GEN.1041)

The wind that strips a rocky planet under about 0.1 Earth mass with a dayside over 2000 K (`physics/disintegrating_planet.py`): the vapour pressure of olivine, the isothermal Parker wind of the vapour and the full model's lower rate, the share of the time the planet loses mass, and the gate of the oddity. Source: Perez-Becker and Chiang 2013 (arXiv:1302.2147), read 2026-10-30 from a fetched copy whose abstract and sections 1 to 4.7 were verbatim and whose end (4.8 and the conclusions) was a summary; the equations and numbers below are those of the verbatim part. The paper writes the object KIC 12557548 b (KIC 1255b), the same as Kepler-1520 b. Kinds, tiers and tweak levels as in 6.1 and in the list of every stand-in. Not in it: the dust tail (the paper gives the dust loading as a ratio and no length), the transit depth (the observed 1.3 to 0.2 percent), and the optical depth of the dust in the surface temperature (eq. 6 with `tau` set to 0).

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DP1 | D | T2 | L0 | disintegrating_planet.OLIVINE_MOLECULAR_MASS_H 169, OLIVINE_SUBLIMATION_ERG_G 3.21e10, OLIVINE_VAPOUR_CONSTANT_DYNE_CM2 6.72e14: the olivine vapour pressure of eq. 7 | olivine or pyroxene (60 m_H, 9.61e10, 3.13e11); iron is 50 times higher at 2145 K | a dayside of 1800 to 2400 K | a measured vapour pressure of the planet's real crust | the surface composition of the planet read from its stored bulk | Perez-Becker and Chiang 2013, eq. 7 and section 2.1 (read) |
| DP2 | D | T2 | L0 | disintegrating_planet.VAPOUR_MEAN_MASS_H 30 and ADIABATIC_INDEX 1.3: the vapour's mean mass and index (eq. 8); CORE_MASS_FRACTION 0.32 for the radius | gamma 1.0 halves the mass that survives 10 Gyr | silicate vapour | the paper's wind with its own radiative transfer | the vapour of the planet's own mixture | Perez-Becker and Chiang 2013, eq. 8 (read); the radius is the repository's own small-body relation |
| DP3 | T | T3 | L0 | disintegrating_planet.NON_ISOTHERMAL_FACTOR 1/40 and SOLID_ANGLE_SR 1: the full model's maximum rate over the isothermal one, and the wind's solid angle (eq. 27) | 1/40 (section 3.2: "about 40 times lower"); the factor depends on mass in the paper | planets of 0.01 to 0.1 Earth mass | the paper's own non-isothermal solution at other masses | a wind with radiative cooling | Perez-Becker and Chiang 2013, sections 3.2 and 4 (read) |

| DP5 | B | T3 | L0 | disintegrating_planet.DIVIDING_MASS_EARTH 0.11, MIN_DAYSIDE_TEMPERATURE_K 2000 and MAX_PERIOD_D 1.0: the gate of the oddity | 0.1 to 0.11 Earth mass; the full model's divide is about 0.08 | rocky bodies | a disintegrating planet over 0.11 Earth mass or under 2000 K | the module's own lifetime (a body whose lifetime is under its age) | the abstract and section 3.3 (read); the period gate is the design note's |

### 6.74 Stand-ins of atmosphere retention (GEN.355)

The library that answers when a body can hold an atmosphere (`physics/atmosphere_retention.py`): the shoreline ratio of the cumulative insolation over the fourth power of the escape velocity, the verdict and pressure cap it gives, and the Jeans gate on the gases. The exobase temperature, the cold-side vapour curves and the energy-limited rate are the thermosphere and saturation modules' and carry their own rows. Source: the design note (`atmospheres-retention-and-classes.md` section 3) from Zahnle and Catling 2017 (arXiv:1702.03386) and Catling and Kasting 2017, read second-hand; the 13 bodies are tested in `test_atmosphere_retention.py`. Kinds, tiers and tweak levels as in 6.1 and in the list of every stand-in.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| SH2 | P | T6 | L2 | atmosphere_retention.VERDICT_EDGES (0.7, 1.0, 2.5, 30.0): the shoreline ratios under which a body holds a dense, moderate, tenuous or trace air and over which it holds none | none | a body of one star age | a body that holds air at a ratio over 30 | the logistic of GEN.511 | edges placed so the 13 bodies fall on the observed side: Earth, Venus, Titan and Triton dense, Pluto moderate, Mars, Io and Ganymede tenuous, Callisto and Europa trace, Mercury, the Moon and Ceres none |
| SH3 | P | T7 | L3 | atmosphere_retention.PRESSURE_CAPS_PA (none, 1e4, 1e3, 1, 0): the surface pressure each verdict allows, 0.1 bar, 10 mbar, 10 micro-bar | none | pressure caps of a body of one star | a measured pressure over its cap | the supply-limited pressure of the pressure stage | design: Mars holds 6 mbar at a ratio of 1.8 and Pluto 10 micro-bar at 0.78 (the pressure is capped, supply and vapour set the rest) |

### 6.75 Stand-ins of the nightside collapse of a locked planet's air (GEN.1044)

`physics/nightside_collapse.py` says whether the air of a tidally locked rocky planet freezes onto its night side and how much stays: the CO2 critical pressure is the empirical fit of Wordsworth 2015 (0.14 bar at Earth's flux for an Earth-mass planet, read in the paper, Eq. 43 and Table 2), the night side's temperature under a thin air is the paper's radiative estimate (Eq. 10 and 25), and the gas left is the vapour pressure there. Other gases use the same balance with no calibration. The Moon, Mars and the Earth are tests, not inputs. Design: `docs/design/composition/nightside-collapse-model.md`. Lock-free; the wiring is GEN.1051.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| NC3 | P | T1 | L0 | nightside_collapse.KAPPA_M2_KG 5e-5 and COS_ALPHA 0.5 (the grey optical depth of the thin-air night side) | none | CO2 air under about 1 bar | the paper's 125.2 K at 0.1 bar, Earth's flux, albedo 0.2 (our Eq. 25 gives 126.9 K) | a correlated-k night-side temperature | Wordsworth 2015 Eq. 10, 25 and 27, READ; test_nightside_collapse::test_the_night_side_of_a_tenth_of_a_bar_is_the_paper_s_125_k |

### 6.76 Stand-ins of the super-puff radius (GEN.1042)

The gate, envelope range, occurrence and tidal inflation of a super-puff (`physics/super_puff.py`). The radius itself is not a stand-in: it is the envelope law of Lopez and Fortney 2014 (`bulk_structure`) inside its range and the giant tables past it. Sources read on 2026-10-30: Lee and Chiang 2016 (arXiv:1510.08855, body verbatim), Millholland 2019 (arXiv:1910.06794, body verbatim, equations from plain-text extraction), Libby-Roberts et al. 2025 (arXiv:2505.21358, abstract and sections to 4.1 verbatim) for Kepler-51 d, Piaulet et al. 2021 (arXiv:2011.13444, to section 6.1 verbatim) for WASP-107 b. Not found in any of them: a fading law of the radius with age (the fade is the envelope law's age term plus the XUV loss of `envelope_escape`), the masses and radii of Kepler-51 b and c (Masuda et al. 2024 was not fetched; b is from Lee and Chiang's quote of Masuda 2014), WASP-107 b in Millholland, and any occurrence statistic.

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| PF1 | B | T4 | L0 | super_puff.MAX_AGE_GY 1, with MAX_MASS_EARTH 10, PERIOD_RANGE_D (10, 100) and DENSITY_RANGE_G_CM3 (0.03, 0.1): the gate | Lee and Chiang's class is 2 to 6 Earth masses and under 50 days; the design table is 10 Earth masses and 10 to 100 days | rocky and gas-dwarf planets with an envelope | a super-puff older than 1 Gyr or heavier than 10 Earth masses | the age-dependent radius of a measured sample | the design table (weird-worlds.md W15), Lee and Chiang 2016 Introduction and Sec. 4 (read), Kepler-51 d 0.038 g/cc (read) |

### 6.77 Stand-ins of the helium enrichment of a thinning envelope (GEN.1045)

The step `envelope_escape` lacks: it follows the mass of a hydrogen-helium envelope and keeps it solar, so no air it leaves is helium-rich (audit of 2026-10-30; `physics/helium_fractionation.py`). Sources read on 2026-10-30: Hu, Seager and Yung 2015 (arXiv:1505.02221, equations 9 to 15 and Sec. 2.1, body verbatim, equations from plain-text extraction) and Malsky, Rogers, Kempton and Marounina (arXiv:2309.16054, appendix A, body verbatim; the fetched text shows no journal or year). The crossover mass is in neither paper; the diffusion-limited flux replaces it. Not found: a closed form for the ion-neutral collision frequency in b' (Schunk and Nagy 1980 is cited, not read), the transonic reduction factor `f_r`, and a helium mass fraction as a function of loss share. The module takes the total loss from `envelope_escape` and does not change it, so what it gives is the composition only; the design's validity bound (hydrogen mass fraction under 1e-3) is not applied.

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| HF1 | D | T2 | L0 | helium_fractionation.DIFFUSION_A_CM 1.04e18 and DIFFUSION_EXPONENT 0.732: the binary diffusion coefficient of hydrogen in helium | none (both papers quote Mason and Marrero 1970) | 3,000 to 10,000 K | a measured diffusion coefficient at the homopause | the table of Mason and Marrero 1970 | Hu et al. 2015 eq. 9, Malsky et al. 2023 A3 (read) |
| HF2 | T | T3 | L1 | helium_fractionation.HOMOPAUSE_TEMPERATURE_K 1e4, IONISED_SHARE 0.1 and HOMOPAUSE_RADIUS_OVER_TRANSIT 1.1: the homopause; and b' = b / (1 + x), which gives the paper's 8.0e20 cm^-1 s^-1 at 1e4 K and x = 0.1 | T 3,000 to 10,000 K (the weaker fractionation is at the higher T); x 0 to 0.5 | planets of 4 to 20 Earth masses with a thin envelope | the ion-neutral frequency of Schunk and Nagy 1980 in the full b' | b' from the ionisation fraction at the homopause at each step (Malsky et al. 2023 A3) | Hu et al. 2015 Sec. 2.1; Malsky et al. 2023 A.1, A.4 (read); the form 1/(1 + x) is a fit to the one value of Hu |
| HF3 | B | T3 | L0 | helium_fractionation.SOLAR_ENVELOPE_HELIUM 0.245 and MIN_HELIUM_FOR_ENHANCED 0.4: the starting helium share and the line of the enhanced planets | start 0.24 to 0.27 | hydrogen-helium envelopes | a measured primordial envelope helium | the solar mix of the star's own composition | Malsky et al. 2023 Introduction and A (X 0.74, Y 0.24) (read) |

### 6.78 Stand-ins of the giant-impact tilt of an ice giant (GEN.1053)

The tilt an impact gives an ice giant, the planes that follow it, and the rate (`physics/giant_tilt.py`). Sources read on 2026-10-30: Kegerreis et al. 2018 (arXiv:1803.07083, full text verbatim: impactors of 1, 2, 3 Earth masses on 14.536 minus the impactor, speed at infinity 5 km/s, angular momentum 1 to 10e36 kg m^2/s, no pre-impact spin, atmosphere at least 90 percent kept, debris clouds "quite spherical") and Morbidelli et al. 2012 (arXiv:1208.4685, full text verbatim: impulsive tilt, at least two giant collisions, an earlier obliquity of 4 to 8 degrees or more for prograde moons, quoting Slattery et al. 1992 for an equatorial debris disk of 1 to 3 percent of the mass). Not found: the final obliquity of any run (Kegerreis computes the spin, not the obliquity), a distribution of impactor masses or of the impact parameter, and a rate for tilts over 60 degrees; the "one in five" is the design table's count of the Solar System. Slattery et al. 1992 itself was not read.

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GT1 | D | T2 | L0 | giant_tilt.DEFAULT_IMPACT_SPEED_KM_S 5: the speed at infinity; and the angular momentum of an impact at contact `mu v_c R_c sin(theta)` | 1 to 9 km/s (their test: no significant change) | ice giants of 5 to 30 Earth masses hit by 0.3 to 3.6 Earth masses | an impact simulation of the same masses with a different angular momentum | the angular momentum of the SPH runs of Kegerreis et al. 2018 as a function of b | Kegerreis et al. 2018 Sec. 2.3 and Appendix B (read); contact at the sum of the radii is the module's |
| GT2 | D | T4 | L1 | giant_tilt.MOMENT_OF_INERTIA_FACTOR 0.225, RETAINED_SHARE 0.9 and PRE_IMPACT_SPIN_BREAKUP 0.2: the planet's moment of inertia, the share of the angular momentum it keeps and the spin before | I 0.22 to 0.24; kept 0.8 to 1; spin 0 (Kegerreis) to 0.3 | Uranus and Neptune | an interior model of a given ice giant, or a run that measures the debris angular momentum | the Helled et al. 2010 interior models and the debris of an SPH run | Kegerreis et al. 2018 Sec. 2.1, 3.4 (read); I is recalled, not read |

### 6.79 Stand-ins of the Hycean box (GEN.963)

The four ranges of a Hycean candidate (`physics/hycean_candidate.py`), a flag derived from mass, radius, equilibrium temperature and density on the classes T, I and V. The ranges are the item's (GEN.963, after Madhusudhan et al. 2021); that paper was not read in this build, so the ranges are a quoted definition and not a verified one. The hydrosphere state `hycean` (`hydrosphere.py`, a liquid surface ocean under air over half hydrogen) and the envelope survival (`envelope_escape`, GEN.822; `atmosphere_retention`, GEN.355) are not changed; the flag only names the planets the box selects.

### 6.83 Stand-ins of the scatter step of an outer disc (GEN.1089)

The rule that shares an outer disc's mass between the cold, hot, resonant, scattered, centaur, detached, trojan, ejected, implanted and captured-moon populations (`physics/scatter.py`; design `belt-origins.md` section 6). No source was opened for this module: the design's own [R] numbers (the chaotic zone of Wisdom 1980, the minimum-mass nebula, Malhotra resonance sweeping, Safronov ejection) are recalled, and every share is a stand-in, as the design says (tier educated guess from physics, L1 to L3, one registered row each). Not found: any measured share of a disc scattered, ejected or implanted by a given giant; the test line "scattered 82 percent of the disc" is the design's sentence for an 18 to 30 AU migration, not a source. ### 6.102 Stand-ins of the smoothed rogue density (GEN.1115)

The smoothing of the star density a rogue planet is drawn from (`galaxy/rogue_density.py`; design `rogue-planet-drift.md` sections 3.3 and 5). The widths are the rms offsets `rogue_drift.drift` gives for the design's stand-in kick distribution (median 3 km/s, ln-sigma 0.8, isotropic, 1 to 10 Gyr), so they are only as good as that distribution (GEN.1113 replaces it); the design note's first guess of 0.5 kpc radially is replaced by the computed 190 pc (the note's own range is 0.1 to 0.7 kpc). Not read: any survey of free-floating planet positions.

### 6.116 Stand-ins of the resonance and chaotic-zone map of a belt (GEN.1062)

The zones a belt keeps of its giants (`physics/resonance_gaps.py`; design `belt-evolution.md` sections 2.3 and 2.4, sources `belt-sources.md` BR1 to BR3). The chaotic-zone and resonance-width laws are read in Mustill and Wyatt 2012 (arXiv:1110.1282); the drift is Bennu's, scaled (Vokrouhlicky et al. 2015). Not read: Wisdom 1980 and 1982, Murray and Dermott's resonance widths for higher orders, Wyatt et al. 1999 for the forced eccentricity. The Solar System's Kirkwood gaps and Neptune's zone are test targets only.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

### 6.117 Stand-ins of the history of a belt (GEN.1064)

The events that act on a belt and the dust that falls on a white dwarf (`physics/belt_history.py`; design `belt-evolution.md` sections 2.5 and 4, sources `belt-sources.md` BR4, BR5, BR12 to BR14). The survival after a giant event is read (Walsh et al. 2011, Morbidelli et al. 2015); the lowest accretion rate on a polluted white dwarf is read (Koester et al. 2014). The share of a belt thrown inward by a star's mass loss has no source: none was found that gives a belt (Debes and Sigurdsson 2002 give it for planets). The fractions of white dwarfs polluted are checks on the population, not inputs.

### 6.121 Stand-ins of the Lagrange trojans (GEN.1149)

`physics/trojans.py` says what may sit at L4 and L5 of a pair (host, secondary): the mass rule, the libration and the label. It sets no rate. Report: `reports/lagrange-trojans-2026-10-30.md`.

| TJ2 | P | T2 | L0 | trojans.stable: the secondary plus the trojan under MU_CRIT of the three masses | 0.035 to 0.042 | a trojan of planet mass or more | an N-body run of a pair at 3 percent whose trojan escapes in 1 Gyr | an N-body map of the generator's own pairs | TROY III (arXiv 2407.04677, abstract READ) and research/oddity-rates.md WR10; Cresswell and Nelson 2009 (arXiv 0811.4611, READ) find pairs stable for 1 Gyr |
| TJ3 | D | T2 | L0 | trojans.libration_period, trojans.TADPOLE_LIMIT_DEG 23.9: the slow libration period of the linearised problem and the tadpole to horseshoe limit | limit 23 to 25 | small-mass trojans | a librating body whose period differs from the law by over 10 percent | none | Murray and Dermott 1999 ch. 3 (recalled, not opened) |

### 6.126 Stand-ins of the roundness engine and the binding energy (GEN.1158)

`physics/roundness.py` works out the yield strength of a body from its composition and the roundness ratio (mean radius over the round radius `sqrt(sigma / (K G rho^2))`); the strengths and rules are in `physics/data/roundness_strengths.json`, so measured values and new rules drop in without changing the interface (ice fraction, metal fraction, porosity and an optional shape-set temperature in; strength, round radius, ratio out). `physics/binding_energy.py` gives the gravitational binding energy of every kind of mass object (a black hole stores -1, meaning infinite). Reports: `reports/round-shape-minimum-mass-2026-10-31.md`, `reports/planetary-material-strength-2026-10-31.md`. The bodies in `tests/test_roundness.py` (Ceres, Vesta, Mimas, Proteus and others) are test targets, never inputs.

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| RN1 | D | T1 | L0 | roundness.SPHERICITY_FACTOR 1.1: the longest axis of a body counted round is at most this times its mean radius | 1.05 to 1.2 | every solid body | a body counted round whose axis ratio is far over 1.1 | the same law with a measured shape tolerance | Lineweaver and Norman 2010 (arXiv 1004.1091, READ in full), Eq. 9 |
| RN2 | D | T2 | L1 | roundness.ICE_STRENGTH_PA 1 MPa and roundness.ROCK_STRENGTH_PA 16.5 MPa (from roundness_strengths.json): the default strength of ice (the creep strength of pure ice near 158 K) and the strength that rounds rock at 300 km and 2500 kg/m3 | ice 0.4 to 3 MPa; rock 4 to 30 MPa | ice and rock bodies | a measured body-scale strength far from either | measured body-scale strengths of ice and rock | Lineweaver and Norman 2010 Fig. 2 (Mimas, Hyperion, Pallas, Vesta, Juno); Durham, Kirby and Stern 1997 for the ice value; rock 16.5 MPa also from Hanssen and Hoek rock-mass tables, Simon and others 2009, Slyuta 2017, Pohl and Britt 2020 (lab 100 to 200 MPa scaled down by 10), Leinhardt and Stewart 2009 (weak rock 1 MPa, strong 10 MPa; lab tensile 100 MPa falling to 1 to 10 MPa at km scale), Basri and Brown 2006 (rock round near 400 km radius), Thomas 1989 (icy satellites under about 150 km radius irregular); the rock value is the dry olivine creep strength near 1100 to 1150 K (RN11); `reports/planetary-material-strength-2026-10-31.md` sections 6 and 7, `reports/papers-vs-standins-2026-10-31.md` sections 2 and 4 |
| RN3 | P | T6 | L3 | roundness.MIXING_RULES exp-volume, exponent 2, tested_to 0.56, close_packing 0.65: ice strength times exp(2 f) for the solids' volume fraction f, continued to 0.65 then geometric to the solids' strength at 1 | exponent 1.5 to 2.5; the leg from 0.56 to 1 is an extrapolation | mixed rock and ice bodies | a rock and ice body above 56 percent solids whose measured strength follows another curve | a measured strength against solid volume fraction above 0.56 | Durham and others 1992 as printed in Durham, Kirby and Stern 1997 eq. 3 (READ), measured to 0.56; Durham and Kirby 1988 (NASA TM 4130, READ); the 0.65 packing is recalled |
| RN4 | P | T2 | L1 | roundness.METAL_STRENGTH_PA 100 MPa | 50 to 250 MPa | iron and nickel bodies | a measured strength of a cold iron body | a measured value at body scale | Pohl and Britt 2020 (READ): iron meteorite yield 320 to 450 MPa, kamacite single crystal 134 MPa at 300 K, divided by 2 to 6 for inclusions and cracks; `reports/planetary-material-strength-2026-10-31.md` section 5 |
| RN5 | P | T6 | L3 | roundness.POROSITY_RULES: `none` in use (a void only lowers the density); `exponential` strength * exp(-b phi), b 5, is built and off | b 4 to 9 | porous bodies | a porous ice-rich body whose roundness the rule gets wrong (Proteus comes out round with `exponential` on) | the rubble-pile branch (friction plus a little cohesion, no size threshold) | Pohl and Britt 2020 (READ) find no clear strength trend with porosity in meteorites; the exponential rule rests on recalled ceramics coefficients (Pabst 2015); comparisons of the Ryugu boulder (Grott 2019: 28 to 55 percent porosity, tensile 0.2 to 0.28 MPa) with ordinary chondrite (Slyuta 2017, 10 percent, 18 to 31 MPa), CM (Pohl and Britt, 12 percent, 5.4 MPa) and Tagish Lake (0.8 MPa, about 40 percent) give b of 7 to 9, at fragment scale, so the range is widened to 4 to 9 (`reports/papers-vs-standins-2026-10-31.md` section 5) |

| BE3 | D | T1 | L0 | binding_energy.POLYTROPE_INDEX_CONVECTIVE 1.5: a fully convective star, a brown dwarf and a white dwarf are n = 3/2 | none | convective stars, brown dwarfs, white dwarfs below the relativistic limit | a heavy white dwarf (under by up to 43 percent near the Chandrasekhar mass) | a relativistic white dwarf structure factor | Lane-Emden; Chandrasekhar 1939 |

### 6.127 Stand-ins of the high-gravity class line (GEN.1136)

The surface gravity from which a world with air is class V, a heavy super-Earth, and below which it can be class M or another class (`physics/data/planet_classes.json` row V). It was 1.25 g, about 1.14 Earth radii, so most planets the literature counts as Earth-sized in the habitable zone fell into V and class M was almost empty (0 of 3,164 bodies in one sweep, 1 of 1,788 in another). Bryson et al. 2021 (arXiv:2010.14812) count rocky planets of 0.5 to 1.5 Earth radii as Earth-sized; the rocky mass-radius law puts 2 g at about 1.6 Earth radii. Boss, 6:26 PM EDT 10-30, set the line to 2 g (report reports/class-m-investigation-2026-10-30.md, option 1).

| fill_id | kind | tier | tweak | constant | knobs | validity | falsifier | replaced_by | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GV1 | P | T2 | L1 | planet_classes.json class V: gravity_g min 2.0 (a body with air at 2 g or more is class V; below it the other rows decide) | 1.5 to 2.0 g (Boss left the choice; 1.5 g is about 1.3 Earth radii) | terrestrial bodies with air | a measured occurrence of rocky planets above 1.5 Earth radii with thin N2 and O2 air at a temperate surface, or a habitability model that scores 1.5 to 2 g as unsafe | the high-gravity rows of the habitability index (GEN.646 family) once they read the finished body | Bryson et al. 2021 limit of 1.5 Earth radii for Earth-sized planets (read, per the class M report); the 2 g value is chosen from it by the rocky mass-radius law, not measured |

### 6.129 Stand-ins of the strengths of the ice types (GEN.1177)

The strength of the other ices of an icy body next to ice Ih (`physics/ice_types.py`, `physics/data/roundness_strengths.json` under `ice_types`): the high-pressure phases of water, methane clathrate hydrate, solid carbon dioxide and ammonia dihydrate, and the thresholds that name a body's ice type from its components. Nothing in `roundness.py` reads them yet; the engine takes `ice_types.strength_ratio` as a factor on its ice strength when the types are wired (`reports/planetary-material-strength-2026-10-31.md` sections 2b, 9, 11c, 14).

### 6.122 Stand-ins of the albedo colour of the star (GEN.912)

How the Bond albedo range of an ice-covered or ocean world follows the colour of its star (`physics/albedo_color.py`, read by `climate_draws.albedo_range`). Ice, snow and the clouds over an ocean reflect the visible light of a hot star better than the near-infrared light of a cool one, so a K or M dwarf planet is warmer than its G twin at the same flux. Shields et al. 2013 (arXiv:1305.6926) give three measures that disagree for M by 0.62, 0.79 and 0.93 (Table 2's 1-D snow row, the freezing lines, the GCM mean albedos); Joshi and Haberle 2012 (arXiv:1110.4525) give snow 0.8 and ice 0.5 in the Sun's light and 0.6 and 0.3 in 3300 K black-body light. The dry range is not shifted: the table's land entry is rock under an Earth-like air, and a dry body has none of that to lose.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| SAB2 | P | T6 | L1 | albedo_color.WET_SHARE 0.5: the share of the ocean row's shift the wet range (ocean under cloud) takes | 0.5 to 1 (clouds are grey, the sea is not) | ocean worlds | a cloud-resolving model of an M dwarf ocean planet's Bond albedo | the cloud albedo drawn before the temperature (GEN.414) | the ocean row of Shields et al. Table 2 is the surface under an Earth-like cloud deck; snow is the surface that loses most |
| SAB3 | D | T3 | L0 | albedo_color.SPECTRAL_TEMPERATURES_K 3410, 5040, 5770, 6900: the effective temperatures of the M3V, K2V, G2V and F2V stars | the types' range (about 100 K) | any star | the temperatures of HD128167, HD22049 and AD Leo | the stars' own catalogue temperatures | main-sequence values of the types, recalled; the paper gives none |

### 6.145 Stand-ins of the cluster maximum mass and cloud-shock disruption of the central ring (GEN.1195)

The most massive cluster a ring of gas forms and the passing clouds that disrupt its clusters (`physics/cluster_maximum_mass.py`, `physics/open_cluster_evolution.cloud_shock_mass_lost_sol`, `galaxy/core_gas.py`; report `reports/gen589-cmz-clusters-2026-10-31.md`). The relations are read in full (Reina-Campos and Kruijssen 2017, arXiv:1704.00732, Eqs 1 to 11, section 5 and footnote 9; Gieles et al. 2006, arXiv:astro-ph/0606451, Eq 24; Kruijssen et al. 2014, arXiv:1303.6286, sections 3.1.2.6 and 3.1.3). The observed Arches and Quintuplet (2e4 and 1e4 solar masses) are a test target only. The ring's gas surface density is a stand-in until a source for the gas of a generated ring is read; the maximum goes as its cube.
